#120. Public service union refuses to back Labor at Victorian election, condemning job cuts and $1.2bn consultant spend

Exclusive: Community and Public Sector Union denounces ‘privatisation by stealth’, joining growing number of unions turning on Carroll government

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Victoria’s public servants union says it will not support Labor at this month’s state election, condemning the government for cutting public service jobs while spending $1.2bn on consultants.

The Community and Public Sector Union (CPSU) will announce the decision on Wednesday, joining several other unions that have refused to back Labor or are actively campaigning against the government ahead of the 28 November poll.

Although the CPSU’s Victorian branch is not formally affiliated with Labor, it has worked closely with the party in the past. But its secretary, Jiselle Hanna, told Guardian Australia it can no longer support the government.

“If Ben Carroll’s government won’t commit to no job cuts in the public service, then our union cannot support him,” Hanna said.

The Labor government has made successive cuts to the Victorian public service (VPS), a smaller subset of the public sector that is largely administrative, as it seeks to rein in employee expenses – the state’s largest area of expenditure.

This included cutting about 1,000 VPS roles including more than 300 executive positions, and merging several government entities following an independent review, led by Helen Silver, last year. The government has said the changes delivered $4bn in savings, while frontline roles weren’t affected.

As of June 2025, there were some 58,000 employed in the VPS – compared with the almost 400,000 in the total public sector.

Hanna said since the Silver review there had been more than 200 further restructures, many of which had resulted in “redundancies, job losses and pay cuts”.

“The public service used to be a reliable and secure place to build a career – that job security doesn’t exist any more, it’s been replaced with precarious employment,” she said.

Meanwhile, the Coalition has committed to implementing an indefinite hiring freeze across all back-office public service roles if it wins government, saving $22bn over a decade. The opposition has said the hiring freeze would remain in place until VPS staffing levels returned to the population-adjusted equivalent of 2014–15 – when it was last in government.

Hanna said both Labor and the Coalition’s distinction between frontline public sector roles and back-office VPS roles overlooked work performed by public servants including directing firefighters during bushfires; analysing DNA, fingerprints and ballistics; developing school curricula and investigating childcare centres.

She said while the union accepts “budget savings need to come from somewhere”, cuts to the public service would only lead to increased use of consultants, which she described as “privatisation by stealth”.

Analysis released by the union, based on Victorian government annual reports since the 2017-18 financial year, showed Labor has spent $1.2bn on consultants across 10 departments. The Department of Treasury and Finance had the highest spend of about $222m since 2017, while the Department of Energy, Environment and Climate Action had the highest annual spend of about $86m in the 2024-25 financial year.

The CPSU has launched a campaign urging Victorians to sign a pledge supporting the public service and committing not to vote for candidates who propose cutting jobs. While the campaign does not endorse any political party, it highlights that both the Greens and the Victorian Socialists oppose public service job cuts and the privatisation of public assets.

Last week, Guardian Australia reported the Electrical Trades Union (ETU) state council resolved not to run a statewide campaign for Labor, despite spending more than $1.6m supporting the party at the last election.

On the same day, the United Firefighters Union – which has been embroiled in a wages dispute with government since 2021 – launched its ad campaign urging Victorians to “put Labor last”.

The Australian Services Union is also running ads calling the premier a liar, as part of its long-term campaign to lift the 1.5% rate cap on councils, which restricts pay rises for workers below inflation.

Members of the Australian Education Union, which is not formally affiliated with the Labor party, have also said they won’t be campaigning for the party. The Health and Community Services Union, meanwhile, has eight members running this election – though none for the Labor party it is affiliated with.

#117. Two Australians confirmed dead in Spain’s high-speed train crash

Legal body handling identification says three latest victims include two Australians and a Japanese citizen

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Two Australians are among 69 people who died in last week’s high-speed train collision after Spanish authorities confirmed the identities of three more victims.

A legal body set up to handle the identification work, made up of police and forensics experts, announced in a statement late on Monday the three latest confirmed victims – two Australians and a Japanese citizen. They have not been named.

In a statement on Wednesday morning, Australia’s Department of Foreign Affairs and Trade confirmed it was providing consular assistance to the families of two Australians who had died.

“We send our deepest condolences to the families at this difficult time.”

On Tuesday evening the foreign affairs minister, Penny Wong, said she sent her “deepest condolences on behalf of Australia” to her Spanish counterpart, José Manuel Albares, and to the people of Spain following the “devastating” train collision.

“Our thoughts are with those who have lost loved ones, those injured and all affected by this tragedy.”

Wednesday’s disaster – one of Europe’s deadliest rail accidents this century – has resulted in victims’ relatives enduring an agonising wait as forensics teams identify the dead, sometimes from individual body parts.

The victims include 54 Spanish nationals and 15 foreigners – four US citizens, two Australians, two Japanese and one each from Argentina, Canada, France, Mexico, Portugal, Tunisia and the Czech Republic.

On Friday, Dfat confirmed it was providing consular assistance to the family of two Australians in connection with the crash.

The accident was Spain’s second high-speed train collision this year and raised fresh questions about the country’s rail safety, after a crash in the southern region of Andalusia in January killed 46 people.

The latest disaster struck when a service by state operator Renfe ploughed into the back of a stationary train belonging to Spanish company Iryo outside the central village of Urda, about 130km (80 miles) south of Madrid.

The failure of an automatic safety system halted all trains on a 63km stretch of track, but a control centre authorised the Renfe service to resume its journey while the other train was still.

Labor MP Jason Clare said on Thursday accidents of this kind were “something people never, ever recover from”.

“The whole country is in shock at the moment, seeing these images on our screens. It’s just horrific,” he told ABC TV.

#111. Choice Shonky awards 2026: From ticket sellers and Virgin flight credits to a 25-hour laundry cycle

Australia’s worst products and services for the year have been named by consumer advocacy group Choice

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Concert tickets that surge to $1,650, an airline pocketing $75m of unused flight credits and a washer/dryer cycle that takes 25 hours have all made the dishonour roll in consumer advocacy group Choice’s annual Shonky awards.

Now in their 21st year, the Shonkys spotlight Australia’s worst products and services as decided by Choice.

Choice awarded Virgin a Shonky gong for imposing strict deadlines on customers to use credits issued for cancelled flights between 2020 and 2022, amid Covid-19 travel restrictions. Customers had to book their trips by 30 June this year, and had to use the credits for flights taking off before 27 May 2027. This resulted in $75m of credits going unused and then being returned to Virgin’s bottom line, according to Choice.

A spokesperson for Virgin Australia said: “More than 93% of Covid credits were redeemed by guests, supported by strong bookings over the past four years.

“Following a number of extensions between 2023 and 2025, we regularly communicated the expiry date and later extended the fly-by date to 27 May 2027, giving guests almost another year to travel.”

Ticketmaster and Ticketek received Shonky awards for using what Choice deemed “sneaky” tactics – dynamic pricing and “in demand” seats – to unfairly inflate prices for live events amid the broader cost-of-living crisis.

Dynamic pricing – common in transport and hotel sectors, but a more recent phenomenon for live events – describes products whose prices fluctuate based on demand.

For example, when a popular concert goes on sale, ticket prices may go up as more customers join the online queue.

Choice said John Farnham fans who logged on to Ticketek looking to secure tickets to a tribute concert earlier this year found themselves facing variable prices. “Ultimate” tickets – the most premium option available, which were not subject to variable pricing – cost $1,650.

Similarly, NRL supporters who bought tickets to some matches in 2026 through Ticketmaster reported “in demand” seats selling for $50 more than regular seats, even though they were only one row and one bay apart.

A spokesperson for Ticketmaster said it did not use surge pricing or algorithms that automatically increase prices in response to real-time demand, and that prices were set by event organisers.

“For ‘in demand’ tickets, event organisers set the prices of sought-after seats closer to market value and may review these over the sales period,” they said. “These prices do not automatically increase because more fans join a queue.”

Guardian Australia contacted Ticketek for comment.

The Albanese government promised to restrict dynamic pricing in 2024, but did not include any prohibitions against the practice in its new laws designed to limit unfair trading practices, due to come into effect in 2027.

Choice’s chief executive officer, Monique Perry, said the organisation wanted to see expanded price-gouging laws to protect consumers.

“With Virgin, we’d love to see consumers get the $75m back or have unlimited time to [use] those flight credits,” she said.

Perry said Choice decided which products to consider for Shonky awards based on consumer feedback.

“Some of these are really serious for consumers and where we really have a call to action and sometimes it’s more about buyer beware,” she said.

Perry said calling out companies sometimes resulted in tangible actions, such as Commonwealth Bank pledging to refund $68m of fees after Choice gave it a Shonky in 2025 for charging $270m in excessive fees to low-income customers.

This year, Hisense got a Shonky for advertising a washer/dryer model as “super energy efficient”.

However, Choice says the energy rating can only be achieved on the 2025 Series 8i heat pump combo washer/dryer (HCF8I1215HB) if one uses its 13.5-hour “eco” dry cycle, and 728-minute wash cycle – more than 25 hours combined for a wash and dry.

A spokesperson for Hisense said: “The energy rating displayed on our product is determined in accordance with the Australian government’s regulated Energy Rating Scheme and the applicable Greenhouse and Energy Minimum Standards (GEMS) requirements. Energy ratings are calculated using prescribed Australian testing standards, established performance criteria and defined assessment methodologies.”

Perry described Hisense’s ratings as “gaming a broken system”. Manufacturers get to nominate which program will be assessed for energy ratings “and it’s usually one that produces the best energy-efficiency outcome, rather than one households would actually use”.

Choice scolded Petcover for its “economy” insurance policy, saying the insurer sold customers an entirely new policy every 12 months, which classifies all conditions their pet has previously experienced as pre-existing, thus excluding them from cover.

Quest received an award for selling its “protein chips” at a huge mark-up compared with similar, non-protein options. For example, at more than $20 per 100g, the Quest nacho cheese protein chips were 886% more expensive than nacho cheese flavour Doritos, which cost $2.06 per 100g.

Although the Quest chips contain 56g of protein per 100g, compared with 7.5g for Doritos, Perry said there were much cheaper and simpler ways to get additional protein.

PlayStation owner Sony received a Shonky for revealing customers did not actually own the movies and games they’d paid for, and instead had only bought a licence to access the content, which can be revoked at any time without a refund.

Choice said the problems with this kind of licensing arrangement became apparent in June, when Sony announced an end to its licensing agreement with Studio Canal in the UK and Europe. This resulted in 500 movies paid for and stored in customer’s digital libraries being removed, without any kind of refund, Choice said.

Guardian Australia contacted Quest, Petcover and Sony Entertainment for comment.

This article was amended on 4 November 2026 to clarify that the $1650 John Farnham tickets were not subject to variable pricing.

#110. Greenland's parliament ratifies Trump's US security agreement

The US is set to expand its military footprint on the Arctic island, part of the Kingdom of Denmark. It follows pressure from Donald Trump earlier in the year which opened a major rift in the transatlantic alliance.

Greenland's parliament on Tuesday signed off on a trilateral security and military-access agreement with the United States and Denmark that paves the way for an expanded US' military presence on the strategically located and mineral-rich island.

The deal has alleviated pressure coming from US President Donald Trump, who earlier this year had repeatedly threatened to take control of the self-governing Danish territory, citing US security interests.

After months of negotiation the agreement was signed in September by Trump, Danish Prime Minister Mette Frederiksen and Greenlandic Prime Minister Jens-Frederik Nielsen on the sidelines of the UN General Assembly in New York.

The agreement allows for two new US military bases and Trump's "Golden Dome" missile defence system. It also keeps Greenland under Danish control, while reaffirming Greenlanders' right to self determination.

On Tuesday, shortly before the vote in the capital, Nuuk, Greenlandic Foreign Minister Mute Egede welcomed the agreement's ratification.

"We must accept that peace and stability are no longer something we can take for granted," Egede said.

In a first debate last month, a majority of Greenlandic lawmakers had already signalled support for the agreement.

Pele Broberg, chair of the pro-independence Naleraq party, questioned the deal's value for Greenland.

"We are looking for a better agreement that we can benefit from when we become an independent state. But no economic benefit has been gained from the agreement," Broberg said.

The agreement will come into effect after ratification by the Danish parliament, which is due to read the bill over two sessions in November. A vote is scheduled for November 19 in Copenhagen.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

In January 2026, Trump began calling for the US to control Greenland, and threatened the use of military force and tariffs against US European allies if he did not get his way.

Trump had claimed that Denmark was not doing enough to defend its autonomous arctic territory from unspecified threats from China and Russia.

Trump's repeated threats damaged US ties with Denmark and rattled the US' European NATO allies.

On January 21, after Trump met with NATO chief Mark Rutte on the sidelines of the World Economic Forum in Davos, tensions cooled after Rutte and Trump announced a "concept of a deal" allowing for expanded US defense access to Greenland, which would later evolve into the agreement signed in September.

Denmark had repeatedly said it was willing to discuss expanding the US military footprint in Greenland, while rejecting Trump's more extreme demand that Greenland become US territory.

Critics have pointed out the same deal could have been made without the extreme disruption caused by Trump's territorial and tariff threats.

Under a 1951 agreement, the US already has considerable military freedom of action in Greenland.

After years of voluntarily drawing down its military presence, the US only maintains the Pituffik Space Base in northwestern Greenland, which is used for missile defense and radar surveillance.

#109. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#104. Beijing Queer film festival cancelled after harassment from authorities

Forums for discussion of feminist and LGBTQ+ views face growing intolerance from Chinese state, say experts

Beijing’s annual independent queer film festival has been cancelled after what is understood to have been a campaign of sustained harassment from authorities.

Beijing Queer film festival, which was due to begin on Friday in China’s capital, is one of the country’s oldest independent film festivals.

In a statement posted on Instagram late on Thursday, the organisers said: “We regret to inform you that the Love Queer Film Week … has been cancelled. We sincerely apologise for any inconvenience and disappointment that the cancellation of this event may cause to those who planned to participate or had been looking forward to the film week.”

Police had increased pressure on the festival through repeated phone calls in recent weeks, it is understood.

Founded in 2001 by a group of Peking University students, the festival grew to become the only film festival in mainland China to offer a public forum for films and discussions exploring LGBTQ+ issues.

The Chinese authorities have grown increasingly intolerant of open discussion of feminist or LGBTQ+ issues. Film festivals in particular attract particular scrutiny because they provide an opportunity to platform narratives that sit outside the Communist party’s worldview.

Beijing Independent film festival, China’s most prominent independent film festival, was closed down in 2014 after the authorities detained the founders and shut off the electricity supply to the venue. Last year, an independent Chinese film festival that was due to be held in New York was cancelled after harassment from the Chinese authorities, an incident which raised concern about transnational repression.

Maya Wang, the deputy Asia director for Human Rights Watch, said: “The Chinese government under Xi has been relentlessly tightening social and ideological control, and so any activities outside of those sanctioned by the party-state are subjected to intensifying scrutiny and often also official harassment.

“The authorities have put more pressure on what little is left of China’s civil society, as well as the LGBT communities and independent film-makers. A queer film festival basically faces all of these overlapping pressures.”

Hongwei Bao, a professor of Chinese studies at the University of St Andrews who focuses on LGBT issues, said that the festival was “a beacon for LGBT resilience” in China.

“The shutdown of the Love Queer Cinema Week has a chilling affect on the Chinese queer community,” Bao said.

In June this year, screenings of films with LGBTQ+ themes that were scheduled to be held in Beijing during Pride month at the Institut Français, a French cultural centre, were cancelled because of police harassment.

Despite the increased pressure, Beijing Queer film festival remains a popular event. Several international film-makers whose work had been selected for the festival had travelled to China to participate. Last year, the festival received 200 volunteer applications, four times the number received in 2024.

Beijing’s public security bureau could not be reached for comment.

#102. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#100. North Korea denies being behind record $6.3bn Binance hack

Kim Yo Jong, the sister of North Korea's leader Kim Jong Un

North Korea has denied claims that it was behind the record $6.3bn (Â£4.7bn) hack of cryptocurrency exchange Binance, calling the allegations "absurd rhetoric".

Kim Yo Jong, a top Democratic People's Republic of Korea official and the sister of leader Kim Jong Un, said on Tuesday that the accusations are another "sinister attempt" by the US to damage Pyongyang's public image.

The US Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI), the Secret Service and cyber analysts have said North Korea orchestrated the hack, which has been described as the largest in history.

This is the latest hack to be linked to North Korea, which has been accused of relying on cyber-crime to help finance its sanctioned economy.

Blockchain analysts and the FBI have said the attack was led by state-backed hacking group TraderTraitor.

The FBI has called on industry partners to help block transactions involving cryptocurrency accounts linked to the group.

"The US has made a trite provocation of linking it with the Democratic People's Republic of Korea again," Kim Yo-jong, the director of North Korea's General Affairs Department, said in a statement.

"It is a grave political provocation against the DPRK that the US misleads the international public opinion as if all the cyber crimes in different parts of the world are our deeds," she said.

Why North Korea's 'elite' hackers are the prime suspect in major Binance hack

Who could be behind the Binance hack and what we know so far

But Pyongyang has been blamed for a number of major cyber attacks in recent years - accusations that it has denied.

The TraderTraitor unit, which is also known as the Lazarus Group, was blamed for a then-record $1.5bn hack of crypto exchange Bybit in 2025.

Pyongyang has systematically built its cyber operations, recruiting promising students from universities and sending them to hacker units overseen by North Korea's intelligence agency, research groups like the S Rajaratnam School of International Studies think tank have said.

The country has consistently denied such claims.

Kim Yo Jong said "it is the consistent policy stand of the DPRK government to resolutely oppose all sorts of malicious acts in cyberspace".

Kim Yo-jong: North Korea's heir apparent?

Who is Kim Yo-jong? Video, 00:02:32Who is Kim Yo-jong?

Binance did not respond to the BBC's queries about North Korea's alleged involvement in the hack.

Since the incident, the crypto giant has largely focused its public statements on the technical nature of the breach and its response to protect users.

The company's spokesperson said that a net $4.72bn has been withdrawn from the exchange since transactions resumed on Sunday.

The net outflow represents a "relatively limited portion" of Binance's total assets, which stand at approximately $157bn, the spokesperson told the BBC on Tuesday.

That figure reflects the impact of the hack, subsequent withdrawals and market price changes.

"Withdrawal levels are stabilising to what we would consider normal and expected on-chain activity," she added.

The company said last week that initial checks indicated that an engineer's laptop had been compromised, enabling an attacker to make unauthorised withdrawals.

Trading in major cryptocurrencies on Binance has since resumed after the exchange worked with issuers to freeze assets impacted by the hack.

The company has repeatedly said it would cover all of the losses linked to the attack and that it has so far covered around 95% of the assets stolen in the breach.

#99. Spain transport minister offers resignation in wake of train accident

After two deadly train crashes this year, Spanish Transport Minister Oscar Puente has said he will step down.

Spanish Transport Minister Oscar Puente offered his resignation on Tuesday in the wake of one of Europe's worst rail disasters in recent memory last week that killed 69 people.

The tragedy, Spain's second fatal rail collision in less than a year, has intensified scrutiny of railway safety and added political pressure on Prime Minister Pedro Sanchez ahead of parliamentary elections on November 29.

In January, a train crash in the southern Andalusia region killed 46 people.

"A transport ​minister ‌in a country like Spain cannot continue in ‌office with two accidents ‌of ​this severity in less than a year," he told lawmakers while announcing his intention to resign.

Puente, member of Sanchez's Spanish Socialist Workers' Party, also said he would withdraw his name from the list of parliamentary electioncandidates. He did not specify when he would leave ​his post.

Puente's political opponents had demanded he step down, while train unions had been unhappy with him for appearing to pin blame for the disaster on one rail control center operator.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Just hours before addressing lawmakers, Puente had presented improved rail network safety measures and told a press conference the government would "work tirelessly to achieve and regain the public's trust in high-speed rail."

This included plans for control center software that would issue automatic alerts and make it easier to spot traffic anomalies, and an AI agent for system oversight to "minimize the possibility of human error."

Another measure Puente mentioned was requiring a second operator confirm decisions made in scenarios involving a faulty automatic safety system.

The accident happened during the failure of an automated safety system that had stopped all trains along a 63-kilometer stretch of track between Madrid and Seville on Wednesday morning.

Despite the stoppage on the line, a control center allowed a high-speed train operated by state-owned rail company Renfe to continue its journey.

The Renfe train collided with stopped train owned by private Spanish company Iryo outside the village of Urda in Toledo province.

#96. How crypto hacking funds isolated North Korea’s nuclear programme

Cryptocurrency theft is estimated to generate half of the country’s foreign currency income

North Korea plays by its own rules. The isolated, secretive and autocratic country is largely cut off from the world’s economic system – but when it comes to generating foreign earnings it relies on some of the world’s most sophisticated hacking.

Experts, such as TRM Labs, quickly attributed the extraordinary $6.3bn (£4.7bn) Binance cryptocurrency theft to TraderTraitor, an elite North Korean hacking unit that has also been known as the Lazarus Group. Though the link may not be proven yet, the pattern of behaviour is familiar.

North Korea has a history of cryptocurrency theft dating back to 2017, focusing on the sector’s relatively lax security controls as a way of stealing money and generating revenues for its military, including its nuclear weapons programme.

Kim Jong-un, its dictatorial ruler, controls a stockpile of about 50 nuclear warheads, and has been supplying troops and weapons to help Russia in its war in Ukraine. But North Korea is also extremely poor: 40% of the population are undernourished according to the UN and nearly one in five of its children are stunted.

A UN panel of experts, reporting to the security council, said in the spring of 2024 that it was reviewing “58 suspected cyber-attacks” by North Korea “on cryptocurrency-related companies between 2017 and 2023” worth $3bn.

The panel said the cash was estimated to generate half of North Korea’s foreign currency income. One unnamed country went further with its estimate: the stolen billions fund “40% of the weapons of mass destruction programmes”.

The suspected crypto hacks amounted to as many as 17 in 2023 alone: thefts valued at $750m, and disturbingly, the sums involved are only increasing. The world’s largest previous crypto hack took place in February 2025, when $1.5bn was stolen from the Bybit exchange – the work, the FBI said, of TraderTraitor.

Ciaran Martin, a former head of the UK’s National Cyber Security Centre, argues that North Korea appears to have targeted crypto because traditional banks have developed sophisticated defences that the fast-growing and lightly regulated sector has yet to match.

“Over the last decade, hackers have walked off with nine- or 10-figure sums from crypto hacks on more than 30 occasions. The number of traditional banks who have suffered a loss of more than $100m as a result of a cyber-attack in the same period is zero or one,” Martin said.

North Korea was behind one of the last large cyber thefts on a traditional bank – an attack on Bangladesh Bank in February 2016, where North Korean hackers tried to illicitly move $951m from an account the bank had with the Federal Reserve Bank of New York, taking advantage of time differences between the two.

A piece of luck prevented most of the money from being sent to an intermediate account in the Philippines. The corresponding bank was based on Jupiter Street, which shares its first word with the name of a sanctioned Iranian ship, so most of the transfers were held up. In the end, $81m was stolen.

The isolated state has long been accused of engaging in criminal activities to survive, including the forgery of high-quality $100 bills, known as “supernotes” in the 1990s, and the production and trafficking of crystal meth for export in the 2000s.

The UK has been rarely targeted. In 2019, North Korean hackers did try to take control of cash machines linked to 56 financial institutions to make them pay out sums well in excess of normal. British officials spotted what was afoot, alerted the banks and the hack was quietly suppressed with no media coverage.

The irony is that the internet in North Korea is severely restricted, with access denied to the general population. Maths and science are pushed at school, however, and at university some of the best students are recruited into hacker groups, such as Lazarus, operating under the auspices of the country’s Reconnaissance General Bureau spy agency.

Hacking is closely tied to the all-powerful state. In December 2014, it emerged, to the embarrassment of Sony Pictures, that film stars Jennifer Lawrence and Amy Adams were paid less than their male counterparts in American Hustle. It was one of thousands of embarrassing emails leaked from the Hollywood studio through a hack.

The attack, by North Korea’s Lazarus Group, was conducted because Sony had funded a film, The Interview: a Seth Rogen comedy that depicted the assassination of Kim Jong-un. Amid the chaos of the revelations, Sony tried to cancel the movie, then had to U-turn on its retreat, releasing it online and to a handful of cinemas, after being criticised for caving in to North Korean pressure.

→ Baca artikel asli

#95. Major test for Australia's social media ban begins in High Court

Australian teen Noah Jones is no longer a plaintiff in the case but will attend Tuesday's hearing

Australia's rush to put in place a landmark social media ban for children led to "ill thought out" reforms which have robbed them of their right to free communication, the country's top court has been told.

In a major test for the world-leading policy, online forum Reddit and a digital rights group argued at a hearing on Tuesday that the law should be struck down as it is unconstitutional.

Introduced last December, the ban is among a number of online safety reforms the Australian government has spearheaded in recent years.

Supporters say it protects children from harmful content, but critics say it is too blunt an instrument and efforts should focus on improving online safety.

Speaking outside the High Court in Canberra, Communications Minister Anika Wells said the question of what society is willing to accept from tech platforms is "the most compelling policy question of our time".

"We are here to defend a fundamental tenet of Australian democracy, which is an Australian's right, and particularly an Australian child's right, to be free from harm," Wells said.

"We seek to defend that from sustained ideological attack."

The constitutional challenge was filed by the Digital Freedom Project a few weeks before the ban started on 10 December last year. The group is led by New South Wales parliamentarian John Ruddick, who told reporters outside the court the policy amounted to "censorship".

Two teens who originally spearheaded the case, Noah Jones and Macy Neyland, have since withdrawn as plaintiffs. The teens have yet to publicly state their reasons for doing so.

But Jones told media outside the court that children deserved to have their voices heard.

"If the children aren't collaborating and sharing their own views, then they're just spectators in their own world," he said, according to Nine newspapers.

The company said it shared Australia's goal of protecting young people online "but there are better and more effective ways" than a ban.

"As we challenge this law in court, we'll continue to comply with it and advocate for more effective youth safety measures," said Reddit's chief communications officer Adam Collins.

Inside the courtroom, the company's lawyer Perry Herzfeld argued the law breached the freedom of political expression implied in Australia's constitution by restricting children from the most important means of communication in the modern world.

He said the ban was "ill thought out" and contradicted the advice of Australia's online safety tsar, and was rushed through without true public consultation - pointing out they had only 24 hours to make submissions about the law.

Meanwhile government lawyer Joanna Davidson SC, began outlining for the full bench of the High Court why the government believed social media harmed children. She will continue presenting her case on Wednesday.

Under the ban, ten platforms - Facebook, Instagram, TikTok, YouTube, Reddit, Snapchat, X, Kick, Threads and Twitch - must take "reasonable steps" to ensure no one under 16 years old can access its site.

Platforms risk fines of up to A$99m (US$70m, Â£52m) for serious or repeated breaches, but since the ban started, no company has been fined.

The eSafety Commissioner - the body tasked with enforcing the ban - has launched several investigations into alleged non-compliance by Facebook, Instagram, Snapchat, TikTok and YouTube.

WhatsApp and online gaming sites like Roblox and Discord were not included in the ban, nor were educational platforms like YouTube Kids and Google Classroom.

In the first few days of the ban, Meta - which owns three of the banned platforms - said it had closed about half a million accounts.

Some children said they were booted off their accounts in the days before and when the ban started - but many have said they were able to easily circumvent the age checks which included uploading credit card details, photo IDs or a selfie.

Australia has banned social media for kids under 16. How does it work?

'I feel free': Australia's social media ban, one month on

#92. Fighting returns to Kherson region after longest Ukraine war ceasefire

Hundreds of Oleshky evacuees arrived back in Ukraine on Saturday after years trapped on the frontline

Fighting has resumed in Ukraine's Kherson after a six-day truce brought quiet to the front-line region for the first time in years.

The ceasefire - the longest since the start of Russia's full-scale invasion in 2022 - allowed hundreds of people trapped in the Russian-occupied Ukrainian town of Oleshky and its surroundings to be evacuated.

On Monday morning, local authorities confirmed that strikes had resumed, saying Moscow had hit a medical facility in Kherson city centre. They also said a 62-year-old woman and a 73-year-old man were injured when a Russian drone attacked the car they were travelling in.

Ukraine also accused Russian forces of using the humanitarian ceasefire to strengthen their military positions, rotate units and deliver ammunition.

"The enemy used the humanitarian pause to set up their positions," said Ukraine's General Staff, adding they had recorded rotation of Russian units, expansion of anti-drone equipment on the roads, and delivery of ammunition.

The Kremlin appeared to corroborate this when spokesman Dmitry Peskov said it was "reasonable to assume that the Ukrainian army also used this period for the necessary regrouping".

"It is a completely standard practice in such situations," he added.

The ceasefire had been brokered by US First Lady Melania Trump. Both Kyiv and Moscow thanked her repeatedly, though no details of her involvement were shared.

An unusual show of cooperation came from Russia's human rights ombudsman Yana Lantratova and her Ukrainian counterpart Dmytro Lubinets. The pair oversaw the ceasefire, travelled into Oleshky and were filmed speaking to residents together. They publicly thanked each other for their work, often highlighting that no violations of the ceasefire had been observed.

Both Lantratova and Lubinets called for the initiative to be repeated elsewhere, and Ukraine repeatedly said it would be prepared to observe similar ceasefires on a national level too.

But many have questioned whether this will lead to more cooperation and further truces.

In fact, the Kremlin swiftly dismissed the idea even as the ceasefire was under way, calling Oleshky "an isolated case" which would not affect the "settlement process" â a euphemism for the stalled peace talks. "There are no similar plans or initiatives," Peskov reiterated on Monday.

And although last week the guns fell silent in Kherson region, elsewhere in Ukraine Russia's air strikes continued, killing scores of civilians and destroying crucial infrastructure.

The decision to observe an unusually broad and comparatively lengthy ceasefire in and around Oleshky was therefore a notable exception.

The ceasefire's observance appears, at least in part, to have been linked to Melania Trump's reported involvement. By violating the truce, Moscow would have risked undermining its own efforts to cultivate the crucial relationship with her husband, US President Donald Trump.

Some commentators have argued that Trump himself would be interested in presenting the Oleshky ceasefire as a foreign policy achievement scored days before the US midterm elections on Tuesday.

"I totally get why our official representatives need to 'play along' with the US first lady on Oleshky," said Artyom Sheynin, a prominent figure on Russian state TV.

"I understand why it's so important to create a 'success story' ahead of the elections for the US president, whose wife 'achieved a ceasefire' in Oleshky."

Kyiv, too, had its reasons to be fully compliant. Although relations with the US and its president are often fraught, Ukraine continues to rely heavily on US intelligence and US-made Patriot air defence missiles to counter Russian aerial attacks. Ahead of another tough winter of war, continued American support is essential to protect Ukrainian skies from Russia's assaults.

But though the ceasefire may have been born out of political calculation rather than purely humanitarian compassion, it also resulted in hundreds of civilians escaping.

There were emotional scenes when hundreds of Oleshky residents arrived in northern Ukraine on Saturday. Many were met by tearful relatives they had not seen in years. To the journalists waiting, the evacuees told of years of hunger and hardship in a destroyed town.

The hundreds of residents who decided to stay behind will receive monthly humanitarian aid deliveries, Lantratova said, adding that decision too was the result of Melania Trump's mediation.

Yet the troop rotations and resupply efforts show that neither side is expecting Oleshky to be a blueprint for lasting respite in the future.

As Kherson came under fire again, Ukrainian drones hit several energy facilities across four Russian regions. In Samara, residents reported hearing explosions and seeing black plumes of smoke rising into the sky as an oil refinery was hit for the fifth time in a month.

If the truce brought a rare moment of relief to Oleshky, it has done little to alter the wider trajectory of a war that grinds on for now.

#89. Binance resumes customer withdrawals after record $6.3bn hack

Cryptocurrency exchange says 90% of losses covered as FBI blames North Korean state-sponsored group TraderTraitor

The cryptocurrency exchange Binance has started to allow its customers to withdraw their funds from the platform, as it recovers from a record $6.3bn (£4.7bn) hack.

The exchange was hit by the theft on Thursday, which has since been described as the biggest heist in history, with experts identifying North Korea as the likely culprit.

Binance initially froze withdrawals as it investigated the breach, but on Sunday announced that it had began a phased reopening after 2pm Singapore time.

In the first phase people holding the ethereum, USDT and USDC tokens on the ethereum blockchain were able to withdraw their funds, as well as some others held on the BNB Smart Chain blockchain. Later on Sunday, dealing in the Dogecoin and Avalanche currencies resumed on their own platforms.

The platform said it had replenished 90% of the ⁠amount stolen. The remaining 10% of tokens were less liquid, meaning they ⁠may take ​longer to buy, the company said in a post on X on Saturday.

Richard Teng, Binance’s co-chief executive, said separately that the company was “committed to covering the ⁠remaining 10%” and users would not bear the financial impact of the hack.

A weekly dive in to how technology is shaping our lives

It came as the FBI on Saturday confirmed earlier suspicions from private investigators and analysts that a North Korea-affiliated group called TraderTraitor was behind the hack.

TraderTraitor is believed to be part of the Lazarus Group, which gained notoriety in 2014 when it was accused of hacking into Sony Pictures as revenge for The Interview, a film that mocked the North Korean leader Kim Jong-un.

“The Federal Bureau of Investigation is releasing information advising the Democratic People’s Republic of Korea (North Korea) was responsible for the theft of more than $6bn in virtual assets from cryptocurrency exchange Binance, on or about October 29,” the law enforcement agency said in a statement.

The FBI added that it had observed “TraderTraitor actors” converting stolen assets into ethereum, and expected those assets to be laundered further by converting them using decentralised finance and instant swap services.

It said it was urging private sector entities “to block transactions with or derived from addresses TraderTraitor actors” were “using to launder these stolen assets”.

Teng said on Friday that the breach was a “complex attack” involving a compromised laptop and malicious software designed to delete evidence of its activity, making it ‌harder to trace and investigate.

Binance said it had set aside about $6.34bn of company funds to cover the losses. “Our commitment remains the same: Binance will fully cover the financial impact of the funds stolen in this incident,” the company said.

The money spent rebuying tokens came from using part of its $1bn secure asset fund for users, a bitcoin reserve it holds to protect customers in the event of security breaches, as well as some ⁠of its futures insurance fund and own corporate treasury, the company said ​on X.

The attack on Binance came as many observers express worries that the ever-expanding capabilities of artificial intelligence models could compromise cryptography and IT security protocols.

Bitcoin plunged nearly 5% after the news of the hack, while ethereum dropped nearly 6% and BNB, native to Binance, tumbled nearly 10%.

The sheer scale of the hack has put a spotlight on the capacity of North Korea – which faces extensive international sanctions over its nuclear and missile programmes – to find illicit means to capture revenue.

#88. Supreme Court rejects Trump's request to stop California counting disputed votes

The US Supreme Court has rejected a last-minute request by the Trump administration asking it to intervene in California's midterm election vote count.

The justice department wanted 20,000 ballots to be set aside that it alleged may have been issued to non-US citizens in the state.

Two lower courts had already rejected the request. State election officials had questioned the federal government's claims - saying the process used to identify alleged non-citizens was inaccurate.

The Monday ruling comes on the eve of the midterms, which will determine which political party controls the US Congress for the last two years of President Donald Trump's second term.

The Supreme Court announced its decision in a one-line order, saying it was denying the justice department's request for an injunction.

"The application for injunction pending appeal presented to Justice Kagan and by her referred to the Court is denied," it reads.

Its means rulings made in lower courts will stand and essentially ends the Trump administration's bid to disqualify the ballots.

California is the most populous state in America with some 23 million people on its voting register. As of Sunday night, more than six million mail-in ballots had already been received, according to the California secretary of state's office.

The justice department sent letters to the California secretary of state and Los Angeles County Registrar last month, demanding they set aside the ballots of 20,000 registered voters whom the Trump administration "believe" are "likely" non-citizens, according to court documents.

About a week later, armed officials from the Department of Homeland Security delivered letters to county officials and employees across California threatening criminal charges if they didn't sequester those ballots.

In a 22-page brief reviewed by the BBC, the justice department maintained that ballots cast by non-citizens could "debase or dilute valid votes".

On Friday, a federal appeals court declined the government's request to segregate the ballots.

California election officials have questioned the accuracy of the government's claims, and have said the list of names the government has provided is riddled with inaccuracies.

They have expressed concern that eligible voters could be disenfranchised and said segregating ballots whilst voting is underway is unfeasible.

What are Americans voting for this election?

The attempts are a part of a years-long campaign by Trump and his administration to fight alleged voter fraud that they believe is widespread, despite research showing that it is extremely rare.

President Trump has repeatedly claimed that Democrats steal elections by allowing immigrants to vote illegally, and he blames much of it on the so-called "mail-in" or postal voting that is permitted in many Democratic states.

Last week in a scathing ruling, district judge David Carter said the government's move in California was indicative of an "ongoing pattern of attempted election interference" on the part of the Trump administration.

The judge added that "never before in American history has the federal government, on the eve of an election, sought to coerce a state and local government to abandon their own constitutional elections procedures and bend to the will of the executive branch".

With additional reporting by the BBC's David Willis

#87. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

#85. Trump to remove Rwanda and South Africa from US trade deal

Last year, Trump accused South Africa of white genocide in a meeting with President Cyril Ramaphosa at the White House

US President Donald Trump has notified Congress of his intention to remove South Africa and Rwanda from a preferential trade programme, from 1 January 2027.

The African Growth and Opportunity Act (Agoa) allows eligible African countries to export thousands of products to the US duty-free.

US Trade Representative Jamieson Greer said in a statement that an annual review had found that both countries were engaging in practices inconsistent with the programme's eligibility requirements.

He added that the Trump administration had additionally notified the two countries of the decision.

The Trump administration cited barriers to US trade and investment, as well as concerns over national security and foreign policy interests in the region.

For South Africa, the US pointed to corruption.

In a letter to South African President Cyril Ramaphosa, Trump said Pretoria's actions on international security and its relations and cooperation with governments of concern to Washington risked undermining US national security and foreign policy interests.

He also accused South Africa of maintaining significant barriers to American trade and investment, including restrictions affecting agricultural market access, regulatory requirements, investment protections and intellectual property.

"The Trump administration is working to ensure our African partners take the necessary steps to meet the eligibility criteria," Greer said.

South Africa's Trade Minister Parks Tau described the decision as "unfortunate", saying Washington was raising new issues that had not featured in trade negotiations between the two countries, which began in 2025.

"We will continue to accelerate the diversification of South Africa's export markets to protect jobs," Tau said.

South African white genocide does not exist, new ambassador to US tells BBC

Is there a genocide of white South Africans as Trump claims?

South Africa has also sought to put the potential impact of losing Agoa benefits into perspective, arguing that the country's broader trade relationship with the US extends well beyond the programme.

Government figures show that about 89% of South African exports to the US entered under ordinary World Trade Organization most-favoured-nation terms in 2025, while around 10% benefited from Agoa, down from 28% in 2024.

"The value of South African exports under the programme fell to about $1.7bn (Â£1.3bn) in 2025, from $4bn the previous year," Tau said.

Bilateral trade between the two countries reached $28.8bn in 2025, while more than 600 American companies operate in South Africa, supporting about 134,600 jobs.

Pretoria says the two economies have complementary trade interests. South Africa supplies 12 of the 50 minerals considered critical by the US, as well as 70% of American isotopes used, among other things, in cancer treatment.

South African manufacturers also use American inputs in products exported to the US under Agoa, including vehicles and boats.

Oxford Economics senior economist Jee-A van der Linde said Washington's move will put further pressure on South Africa's manufacturing sector, warning that affected exports may be difficult to redirect to other markets.

"This decision comes as global trade flows remained disrupted and South Africa faces growing competition from cheap Chinese imports, particularly in the automotive industry," he said.

However, he noted that South Africa had previously managed to redirect some tariff-affected exports to markets including Europe and China.

US Ambassador to South Africa Leo Brent Bozell III said it was regrettable that South African policies led to its termination from Agoa.

"This was a predictable and preventable outcome resulting from the rejection by South Africa's government of real concerns raised by the US," he wrote on X.

The announcement comes amid strained relations between Washington and both countries, with the proposed termination threatening their access to preferential trade arrangements with the US.

Last year, Trump accused South Africa of persecuting its white minority and launched a programme for white Afrikaners to get asylum in the US.

Trump also repeated widely discredited claims of a genocide of South Africa's white population.

In Rwanda's case, Washington also raised concerns about human rights, political pluralism, the rule of law and workers' rights.

The move comes barely a week after Rwanda's defence ministry was hit with sanctions by US Treasury for its role in the armed rebellion in eastern DR Congo.

The US Treasury said Rwanda's defence ministry directed thousands of troops to fight alongside M23 rebels in eastern DR Congo, providing weapons, equipment, logistics and training.

Rwanda has denied backing the M23 despite overwhelming evidence.

Rwanda saw its exports to the US rise to $43.3 million in 2025, up from $13.1 million in 2024, according to US import data.

Alexander Venter, an Oxford Economics economist, said Washington accounted for about 2% of Rwanda's merchandise exports in 2025, with only around 4% of its exports to the US benefiting from Agoa preferences.

Neighbouring African countries and Asian markets continued to dominate Rwanda's trade, Venter added.
