#80. The US-China trade war seems to have subsided – but the sense of calm is misleading

A truce has cooled the trade conflict, but neither country has done anything to limit the outbreak of new hostilities

Donald Trump’s trade wars have largely fallen off the headlines. Fears of new tariffs flare up from time to time when the US president goes off the rails – for instance, threatening to end US trade with countries with which it has a deficit unless the Federal Reserve cuts interest rates. But the sense that the world’s two largest economies are plowing ahead into an overt economic conflagration has taken a back seat to other, more salient fears, about Trump ending American democracy, AI killing us all and stuff like that.

The sense of calm is misleading. Under the radar, the world is marching toward an economic war of potentially epic proportions, one that, history suggests, could well spill over into the real thing.

The truce reached by Washington and Beijing last year in their escalating trade conflict, which has been extended until next January, lowered the heat between the two – putting an end to the escalation of tit-for-tat tariffs and opening a door to some as yet undefined future trade deal.

But neither China nor the US has done anything to establish the mutual assurances that could limit the outbreak of new hostilities. Rather, they are both focused on gaining the upper hand in what seems like an inevitable next round, in a standoff that both sides have defined as a national security priority, essential to ensure economic, technological and military supremacy.

The new arms race is different from those in the past. Rather than building weapons to blow each other up, China and the US are focusing on ensuring the resilience of the international supply chains on which they rely and figuring out how to constrain – and if push comes to shove, throttle – the supply chains essential to the economic and technological progress of their rival.

The paired strategies go back at least to the Covid pandemic, when China’s Xi Jinping called on the nation to “tighten international production chains’ dependence on China, forming a powerful countermeasure and deterrent capability against foreigners who would artificially cut off supply [to China]”.

Early in the Biden administration, national security adviser Jake Sullivan articulated the American goals: to limit China’s access to a narrow set of advanced technologies with military applications without upending the global economic order. He described the strategy as building a “small yard” of forbidden tech surrounded by a “high fence” of trade and investment controls.

By now, the conflict has breached the bounds of these initial rationales. Restrictions on American tech exports, investments and technology transfers to China have proliferated over the last half decade. And they are hitting not only China’s military capabilities but slowing its technological progress writ large. And Trump’s wanton deployment of tariffs is not helping.

China, on the other hand, is becoming increasingly assertive, leveraging its overwhelming dominance over the supply of critical minerals that are essential to hi-tech industries for civilian and military uses. Last year, it stunned the Trump administration by throttling exports of rare earths and magnets made from them to retaliate against US tariffs and efforts to restrict China’s access to high-end semiconductors and the lithography machines needed to make them.

It has relaxed some of these controls, but it maintains many other restrictions. While they have largely been imposed to retaliate against American actions, they have also been used to hit other countries for a variety of policies it dislikes. The stakes are high. The International Energy Agency has estimated that if China were to block the US’s and Europe’s access to rare earth magnets, it could cost their economies about $1.5tn per year.

Where does the supply chain war go from here? The Trump administration is pouring hundreds of millions into projects to mine and process rare earths and other critical minerals, hoping to close China’s lead. It is also doubling down on efforts to limit China’s access to frontier semiconductors and to bar it from buying the machinery needed to make them.

China, on the other hand, has announced a range of hi-tech export restrictions, covering things such as artificial intelligence chips and solar power technology. Moreover, it has threatened to punish countries that work to reduce their dependence on its mineral inputs.

One hopes there is a path to a peaceful entente. This would probably require some shared understanding of what constitutes a legitimate national security risk that may warrant actions to block supply chains. Defining the “yard” is essential. Frustrations of standard commerce should not be dealt with on a wartime footing.

China will probably dominate renewable energy technologies, especially the solar power industry, into the foreseeable future. It may also dominate the electric vehicle industry. This might hurt in Brussels and Washington, but it’s not worth a war. China, on the other hand, will have to accept that its mercantilistic economic strategy is not tenable for the rest of the world. Its combination of massive export growth with zero import growth is not sustainable.

This might seem doable. And yet the odds that the world economy will remain at peace seem slim. One might recall the Axis powers’ efforts to seize territories as the League of Nations curtailed their access to raw materials in the early stages of the second world war. Or FDR’s blocking of sales of American scrap steel and oil to Japan, which probably contributed to Tokyo’s decision to attack Pearl Harbor. Today, American fighter jets, submarines and missiles use rare earth magnets. What would happen if China blocked access to these materials?

The moment is strange. It’s not that long ago that trade was proposed as perhaps the most powerful tool to deliver world peace, building positive-sum interdependence among nations. That’s over. Interdependence has become a vulnerability. As China and the United States fight over who will control the next generation of technology, trade has become a theater of war.

→ Baca artikel asli

#79. Jet may have broken apart before Florida crash, officials say, as family named among victims

Dashcam footage captures Florida private jet crash

Investigators believe parts of a private jet that crashed in Florida and killed all nine onboard may have broken off while it was in the air.

The Learjet 60 had been bound for the Bahamas from Austin, Texas when it nosedived into a multi-lane highway and exploded on impact.

A tech entrepreneur and his young family, as well as the 20-year-old co-pilot, were among those who died on Sunday.

The US National Transportation Safety Board (NTSB) said weather conditions would be a major focus of the investigation but that it was struggling to access the wreckage due to the huge crater created by the jet on the road in Palm Beach.

The plane crashed on Military Trail, a six-lane road that features businesses and restaurants, at around 12:50 local time (17:50 GMT).

"The biggest obstacle that we're going to have is recovering the major wreckage from the impacted crater on the roadway," lead investigator Tim Monville said at a news conference on Monday.

The NTSB said the crater, near the entrance to a mobile home park, had become partially filled with water because of severe storms. Heavy rainfall and thunderstorms had been reported in the area at the time of the crash.

Debris was scattered across sports fields, businesses and homes in the area.

"We do have some evidence that there was some breakup of the aircraft before impact," the NTSB's Tom Chapman said.

"We may find parts of the aircraft some distance away from the actual impact site."

Officials are also trying to find out whether a cockpit voice recorder was onboard at the time of the crash and whether it can be recovered.

Flight analysis indicates that the jet was plummeting hundreds of feet per second before it crashed.

In air traffic control audio recordings, someone in the cockpit can be heard reporting "moderate turbulence" and that the crew was "managing the ice".

They can then be heard saying the plane was "picking up precipitation" and "we need to climb". Moments later, the same voice said the plane was descending at a rate of 10,000ft (3,048m) per minute.

An emergency was declared and they can be heard yelling "we are in an overspeed condition descending 20,000ft per minute", before saying, "we are going to crash into the ground".

Seven passengers died on board, named in a joint statement as tech entrepreneur Adam Robinson, his wife Helen Sharp, and their two children - Emma, four, and Charlie, two - as well as friends of the family Rachael Reed and Charmi Fehr, and nanny Anita Maria Cetina.

The pilot was named in media reports as 55-year-old Jeffrey Gregory, while Nathalia Filgueiras, 20, who earned her pilot's licence when she was 17, was reported to be the co-pilot.

One man in a car on the ground sustained a leg injury.

Tech entrepreneur Adam Robinson and his wife Helen Sharp with their two small children, Emma and Charlie

Robinson and Sharp's family described them as "devoted parents" and said their children were "the light of their lives".

"Helen and Adam were the love affair that will last forever. Their warmth, generosity and gift for making people feel welcome touched so many lives," the statement read.

Robinson described himself on his LinkedIn page as the founder and CEO of RB2B and Retention.com, and the CEO of Moltsets - all technology companies. Helen Sharp was an artist, according to media reports.

Robinson previously worked at the global financial services business Lehman Brothers and the banking group Barclays. The BBC has contacted the companies.

Anita Maria Cetina, Charmi Fehr and Rachel Reed

The joint statement also named nanny Anita Maria Cetina and said she "cared for Emma and Charlie with tenderness and devotion, and she was truly part of the family".

It added: "She showed us a legacy of unconditional love and devotion."

Rachel Reed and Charmi Fehr were described in the joint family statement as "full of joy and adventure".

"They loved their people fiercely, fully and without hesitation. From the moment they met, they were all in on each other and the life they were building."

Nathalia Filgueiras received her pilot's licence when she was in high school

Nathalia Filgueiras's mother, Camila Corteleti, confirmed to US media that her daughter had died in the crash.

Corteleti told the New York Times: "She loved the freedom. It was completely different seeing the world from the top."

Filgueiras had spoken of her pride at receiving her pilot's licence while still a senior in high school in an interview with a local affiliate of CBS News in 2023.

"I like being different than everyone," she said. "Some people think that it's weird that I can fly planes but I can't drive a car - I mean, I can, but a lot of people think it's funny."

A spokesperson for East Coast Jets, where pilot Jeff Gregory used to work, said: "We are deeply saddened to learn that Capt Jeff Gregory was among those who lost their lives in yesterday's tragic aviation accident in Palm Beach."

He was married with two children, US media report.

#78. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#73. Russian bombing kills 31 people at Ukraine aid centre, Kyiv says

Video shows smoke rising from Komyshuvakha blast crater

At least 31 people have been killed after Russian bombs hit an aid distribution centre in Zaporizhia, the Ukrainian regional head has said, describing it as one of the deadliest attacks there since the start of the full-scale invasion.

Mykhailo Semikin said at least nine 3,000kg aerial bombs hit the village of Komyshuvakha in southern Ukraine on Monday and the aid centre collapsed from a "direct hit".

"They had come for help - not to a military facility, not to a frontline position, but to a place where they were supposed to receive support," he wrote on Telegram.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky called the attack "utterly horrific" and said there were still people "under the rubble".

Zelensky posted a video showing a huge crater left after Monday's attack and rescuers searching for survivors.

However, officials say teams have at times been forced to halt their search because of Russian drone attacks in the area.

Zelensky said after the first strike on the local school "used for humanitarian needs", there was a second attack on the place where people were sheltering.

He earlier said that "the Russians could not have been unaware that the local residents who remained in Komyshuvakha received humanitarian aid there every week" on Monday.

The Russian military has not publicly commented.

There are fears that the death toll will rise further as a number of people are still under the rubble

Three days of mourning have been declared in the region.

The death toll has continued to rise on Tuesday. Earlier, when 29 people were confirmed to have died, Semikin said another 10 had been injured and 48 were reported missing.

Among the dead are two rescuers and four police officers, according to Ukraine's state emergencies service DSNS.

Zelensky said the attack was "yet another proof that Russia does not want any de-escalation".

He said that after a recent successful joint Russian-Ukrainian operation to evacuate civilians from the Russia-occupied southern city of Oleshky during a temporary ceasefire brokered by US First Lady Melania Trump, "there was a moment when the next steps could show that peace is possible".

"With such killings, Russia is sending the exact opposite signal - Russia is counting on further killings," Zelensky said.

The Russian strikes left a huge crater where Ukrainian officials say people were sheltering

Russian President Vladimir Putin launched a full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022.

Teams from Ukraine, Russia and the US are due to resume peace talks in Abu Dhabi, the United Arab Emirates, next week, US President Donald Trump's special envoy Steve Witkoff said on X.

Since the talks were suspended in February, Moscow has stepped up attacks as Kyiv virtually ran out of Western-supplied interceptors to defend against missiles.

No major breakthrough is expected at the talks. Witkoff said the discussions would focus on "post-war security arrangements, de-escalation, humanitarian efforts, and next steps toward peace".

Witkoff said US negotiators would also meet European and Nato officials this week in Brussels.

#71. Trump’s troubles with Iran could open the door to China

Beijing launches diplomatic salvo to woo Gulf leaders and bolster its influence in the region

China has held a flurry of meetings with Gulf leaders in a sign that Beijing is stepping up its diplomatic efforts in the Middle East.

The diplomatic salvo comes before a highly anticipated meeting between Donald Trump and Xi Jinping when the US president is expected to attend the Apec conference in Shenzhen, China, on 18 and 19 November.

Xi held talks with Haitham bin Tariq, Oman’s sultan, in a historic state visit to Beijing on Monday.

President Xi’s foreign minister, Wang Yi, met Saudi Arabia’s national security adviser, Musaad bin Mohammed Al Aiban, and the deputy secretary of Iran’s national security council, Ali Bagheri Kani.

Haitham’s trip to Beijing is the first time that an Omani head of state has visited China since the two countries established diplomatic relations nearly 50 years ago. According to a Chinese readout of the meeting, Xi warned that the Middle East should not become an arena for “great power rivalry and geopolitical conflicts”.

He said: “China has always been actively committed to stopping conflict and promoting peace, and hopes to contribute wisdom and solutions to resolving the regional security dilemma.”

Wang said after his separate talks that China was willing to “play a constructive role in helping the region emerge from the shadow of the pandemic and achieve lasting peace and stability”. In his meeting with Bagheri Kani, Wang said China supported Iran “in safeguarding its sovereignty and security”. According to China, the Iranian official said his country looked forward to China “playing a more important role in international and regional peace”.

When the US and Israel launched joint strikes on Iran in February, China was credited with pushing Tehran toward ceasefire talks in Pakistan, bolstering its status as a regional mediator.

Beijing’s influence in the Middle East is “no longer what it used to be”, said Shen Dingli, a Shanghai-based scholar of international relations. But he added: “Given China’s increased investment and trade in the region … its influence cannot be ignored.”

As the war has dragged on, China’s diplomatic efforts have been muted. Its biggest economic leverage over Iran is the fact that it buys about 90% of the Middle Eastern country’s crude oil exports. Between April and June, it slashed its crude imports by 30%, with the total in June plunging to the lowest level in a decade. That saved the global economy from a major spike in inflation. According to some estimates, China’s cut in oil imports shaved $30 or more off barrel prices of Brent crude, a global benchmark.

Beijing has otherwise done little to broker peace in the Middle East. That is partly because it does not see the conflict as being its responsibility to resolve. In March, Wang said the war “should never have happened” and that the US and Israel would find “no popular support” for the strikes.

China is also a beneficiary from the damage that the fighting has done to the US’s global reputation.

According to findings published in July by the Pew Research Center, a Washington-based thinktank, China is now viewed more favourably than the US in a majority of 36 countries surveyed.

The pain caused by the long-term disruption to the strait of Hormuz, the waterway through which much of the world’s oil and gas flows, also poses a risk to China’s economy. Shortages of energy cause inflation, which risks reducing global demand for Chinese goods.

Tehran and Washington are locked in intensive talks to reach an agreement, which could include reopening the strait. Trump has repeatedly threatened to renew military action against Iran after Tuesday’s midterm elections in the US, a move which would cause alarm in Beijing.

Trump’s desperation to reach a deal in the Middle East may also provide an opportunity for China to present itself as a regional peacemaker, bolstering Xi’s status before their meeting. Beijing brokered a detente between Saudi Arabia and Iran in 2023 but since then its diplomatic impact in the region has been limited.

Shen said China’s desire to improve diplomatic relations with Middle Eastern countries as well as the US were “mutually reinforcing” trends.

China’s foreign ministry spokesperson, Mao Ning, said on Monday that China would send special envoy Zhai Jun to Iran, Oman and Saudi Arabia in early to mid-November.

#67. Thousands of European troops to rehearse Ukraine deployment in event of peace

A British paratrooper takes part in a previous Nato training exercise less than 50km (31 miles) from the Russian border in Finland

Britain and France are to lead a military exercise to test a multinational force's ability to deploy to Ukraine, if there is a peace deal.

Nearly 3,000 military personnel from 12 European nations will take part in the rehearsal in neighbouring Poland this month.

Plans for a reassurance force for Ukraine were set out last year as one of the long-term security guarantees being proposed by European nations.

But there are still more questions than answers as to how such a military force might work in practice. Western officials stress that any force sent to Ukraine would not take part in offensive operations or be involved in peacekeeping or ceasefire monitoring missions.

Nor would they be operating under the command of Nato or on the front line.

Officials say their presence would be to provide reassurance to Ukraine and to help rebuild its armed forces.

The US has been involved in discussions, but is not contributing to the mission.

Russia has already voiced opposition to foreign troops being stationed in Ukraine and, last year, President Vladimir Putin said they would be "legitimate targets".

Western officials have declined to spell out the rules of engagement if any international troops were to be attacked in Ukraine. Though they say the force would be able to defend itself.

UK Defence Secretary Wes Streeting said the exercise showed Britain, France and their allies were stepping up preparation for the day when peace came to Ukraine.

He said: "This is not a hypothetical plan, it's a real force, ready to help Ukraine once the fighting stops."

Lt Gen Tom Bateman, the UK commander of the Multi National Force â Ukraine (MNF-U), said the exercise represented an important step in translating political commitments into credible military readiness.

He said it was a "real, capable, readily deployable force that represented a serious commitment from Coalition of the Willing nations to Ukraine's long-term stability and security".

Ukrainian troops take part in urban warfare training at a military base in England in 2023

Exercise Koalicja - which takes place from 9-22 November - will be a rehearsal of how to move, protect and supply a force in Ukraine. It will also be a test of their command and control to ensure troops from different countries can work together.

Some 2,800 military personnel will take part across land, sea and air, with the UK contribution including 300 troops and a pair of RAF Typhoons.

Fighter jets will simulate how they would reassure commercial aircraft once Ukraine's skies can be reopened.

Turkey has agreed to lead the maritime mission, with the eventual goal to clear mines and allow the resumption of trade in the Black Sea.

As well as the UK, troops will come from France, Belgium, Estonia, Germany, Lithuania, the Netherlands, Poland, Spain, Sweden, and Turkey.

However, Western officials could not comment on the final make-up and shape of the force that could be deployed to Ukraine if the fighting stops.

In the past, military experts have suggested that Western nations might have to send tens of thousands of troops to Ukraine to guarantee its security.

Officials acknowledge that the countries' commitment and contributions will depend on the circumstances of a cessation of hostilities and individual nations' political decisions.

It is also unclear what training role Western forces could provide to battle hardened Ukraine.

Ukrainian soldiers are already in high demand training Nato forces - passing on their expertise in drone warfare.

Above all, Russia has shown no sign of wanting to end its war.

#64. Canada and EU announce new alliance open to like-minded countries

Alliance for the Future, unveiled at Montreal summit and open to other countries, conceived for world ‘shaken by new storms’

Canada and Europe have announced a new alliance of like-minded nations to serve as both a “protective canopy under which our citizens can thrive” and a powerful hedge against the unpredictable nature of geopolitics, while welcoming other countries to join.

Speaking to reporters on Friday from Montreal, the prime minister, Mark Carney, and the European Commission president, Ursula von der Leyen, called the new partnership the Alliance for the Future and said the “deepest possible partnership between Canada and the 27-member bloc had the potential to offer unprecedented stability and opportunity”.

“Our societies have been shaken by new storms,” Carney told reporters, referring to the damaging effects of the climate emergency but also the “erosion of democratic norms” and “the weaponization of trade”.

As they concluded their two-day summit, Canada agreed to contribute to a €90bn support loan for Ukraine. Leaders also said future discussions would lead to a more integrated market worth tens of billions of dollars, reduced trade and travel restrictions, and new educational opportunities for both sides.

While a trade war between Canada and the United States has seen Carney go hunting for new geopolitical partners, an over-reliance on American companies – especially for defence – has also given Canada pause.

Last week, Elon Musk throttled the speeds of Starlink and Starshield over Ukraine in mid-October, severely disrupting Ukrainian military and defense operations.

Carney has repeatedly raised the concern that Canada’s defense technologies could be at the whim of political actors or companies with opposing interests. Musk, a dual Canadian citizen, is a critic of Canada’s governing Liberal party and has advocated for provinces to separate.

Carney pointed to a planned merger between the Canadian artificial intelligence company Cohere Inc and Germany’s Aleph Alpha GmbH as a step to lessening reliance on American AI giants. The tie-up will see the newly formed company have dual headquarters in Berlin and Toronto, and Canada’s federal government has announced a stake in Cohere worth up to C$1.6bn.

“We are not waiting for the future of AI to be decided somewhere else by those we don’t know, or by whom we don’t trust,” Carney said on Thursday.

Brussels and Ottawa also announced an agreement on Friday that would bringing Canada into Europe’s supercomputing network, giving Canadian access to European machines to train AI models.

In addition to lofty promises of helping to reshape the global order, or giving safe harbour to like-minded nations looking to weather uncertainty, Canadian and EU leaders also pitched their new, deeper relationship as one that addressed key sources of anxiety for residents, including the cost of living.

“When trade becomes a tool of economic pressure, it’s the families that pay the price, from the supermarket shelf to the cost of a new car,” Von der Leyen said, noting that previously trusted suppliers of energy, critical minerals or semiconductors “are increasingly turned into instruments of pressure”.

“More competition, more choice,” Carney said earlier in the day, suggesting the alliance could find ways to build certainty around supply chains and “withstand shocks, together”.

The leaders once again brought up the prospect of Canada becoming an associate member. The designation is uncharted territory, said Von der Leyen, who added: “We have the ambition, trust, and creativity to shape it together.

“This [associate membership] is a partnership not against anyone else, but for our common strength.”

While the leaders committed to the idea of associate membership, they said a clearer scope of the role would come at a 2027 summit. Some EU members had bristled at the idea of Canada being given a legally meaningless designation that they fear confuses other nations looking for membership. Germany and other states had worked behind the scenes to remove the phrase from a draft of the summit statement. But Von der Leyen, who first floated the term, and Carney both again referred to “associate membership” at the summit.

European Council president Antonio Costa, meanwhile, said earlier that Canada was “by far the most European of the non-European countries”, a sentiment that reflected a belief that despite geography, there was a collective sense of shared values.

Costa said the two sides would pursue “the most closer, the most deepest, the most stronger association ever between European Union and a non-European country”.

Still, the mere fact of the summit has caused friction in the province of Quebec, where the separatist Parti Québécois was recently elected to a minority government. The premier, Paul St-Pierre Plamondon, skipped the event after Ottawa’s refusal to let him speak publicly at it.

“The federal government would have us believe that the premier of Quebec being granted permission to serve shrimp cocktails at a reception constitutes an acceptable and sufficient form of influence for representing Quebec’s interests,” St-Pierre Plamondon wrote in a social media post earlier in the week.

He said an invitation to sit at the head table was the federal government asking Quebec “to get back in line, with the vassalization and invisibilization of Quebec which that implies, in exchange for obtaining better seats to watch the party”.

#62. Asian Cup postponed in wake of attacks on Saudi Arabia

Aston Villa will have goalkeeper Zion Suzuki more available this season because of the rescheduling of the Asian Cup

The 2027 Asian Cup in Saudi Arabia has been postponed because of a series of deadly attacks on the host country.

The 24-team tournament was scheduled to take place from 7 January to 5 February but has been rescheduled to run from 23 December, 2027 to 21 January, 2028.

In October, a missile attack by Yemen's Houthi movement on Riyadh's airport killed 13 people and wounded more than 300.

A statement from the the Asian Football Confederation (AFC), external said the decision to reschedule had been taken "in light of the current exceptional circumstances arising from developments in the region, including substantial logistical complexities and travel disruptions".

It added: "The new dates of the tournament will be presented to Fifa for approval."

The Saudi Pro League has postponed this week's round of fixtures following the rescheduling of the Asian Cup.

It says the rescheduling creates "additional flexibility" in the calendar to allow for a "more balanced distribution of fixtures" across the rest of the season.

A number of events in Saudi Arabia have been cancelled recently, including the Six Kings Slam tennis exhibition event, which was scheduled to be in Riyadh.

The Asian Tour has also indefinitely postponed it's season-ending golf tournament, which was scheduled to take place from 18-21 November.

This is the second successive Asian Cup to be postponed after the 2023 tournament was moved to 2024 and to Qatar from China because of the Covid pandemic.

Saudi minister vows 'serious response' to Houthi attacks on airports

The decision to postpone the 2027 Asian Cup means a number of Premier League players will not have to miss part of the current season.

Some of the players that would have been expected to be called up were Japan's Zion Suzuki of Aston Villa, Wataru Endo, who plays for Liverpool, and Daizen Maeda of Ipswich Town.

Leeds United's Ao Tanaka, Crystal Palace duo Takehiro Tomiyasu and Daichi Kamada, and Hull City's Hidemasa Morita also play for Japan.

Japan would have been one of the favourites for the tournament and Villa, who are in the Champions League, might have had to do without Suzuki for at least seven games if his country had reached the final.

Liverpool are also in the Champions League but Endo has yet to play for the Reds this season.

Brighton winger Kaoru Mitoma is currently injured but is expected to be back later this year and is also likely to have been called up by Japan.

Manchester City defender Abdukodir Khusanov, of Uzbekistan, and Hull City and Australia defender Lucas Herrington would also have expected international call-ups for the Asian Cup.

#59. Trump focused on 'what's important to him': 'Tone-deaf for a normally shrewd populist operator'

Erin Ogunkeye is pleased to welcome Doug Heye, Republican strategist, founder of Douglas Media, and former Communications Director for the Republican National Committee (RNC). As Americans head to the polls, Republicans face a challenge that goes beyond the familiar electoral backlash against an incumbent president. For Heye, Donald Trump's greatest political vulnerability may lie in the widening gap between his rhetoric of economic success and voters' everyday financial anxieties. "For a lot of voters, they see him focused on what's important to him, not what's important to them," Heye explains. Yet he also cautions against interpreting Democratic gains as a sign of renewed confidence in the opposition. Rather than a resurgence of partisan enthusiasm, the elections may expose an electorate increasingly disillusioned with both parties.

#58. Mining giant joins fight to stop killer toad march across Australia

Rio Tinto is ploughing millions of dollars into stopping the poisonous cane toad reaching the Pilbara region of western Australia, where the mining giant has a vast iron ore operation.

The firm, alongside Curtin University in Perth and others, will spend A$28m (Â£14.5m) building a "toad containment zone" to prevent the pest travelling south.

While the cane toad is not a direct threat to Rio Tinto's business, it produces toxins lethal to native wildlife and poses a threat to Pilbara's ecosystem.

Rio Tinto has previously faced criticism for destroying two sacred Aboriginal rock shelters in Pilbara which sat above tonnes of iron ore.

The decision cost the former chief executive his job, provoked a widespread backlash and saw Rio Tinto commit to rebuilding trust with Indigenous communities.

Commenting on the proposed containment zone, Roberta Hunter, a ranger in Australia's Nyangumarta Warrarn Indigenous Protected Area, said: "Cane toads destroy the water and animals.

"When toads come close to our country, we want to be there to stop them because we don't want the country to be destroyed."

The plan is to cut off the toad's water supply by building a barrier across 7,500 sq km (2,895 sq m) of land between the Great Sandy Desert and the Indian Ocean.

In drier months, the cane toad needs water every few days to survive.

If there are no natural water sources, the toad relies on artificial water points normally used for cattle and irrigation.

The funding will be used to stop the toad accessing dams, storage tanks or other ground-level water sources. The five-year project will begin in 2027.

A number of native species are at risk if the toad gets into Pilbara. These include the northern quoll, whose numbers have been decreasing, the goanna lizard and the ghost bat.

The toads were introduced into Australia in 1935 to control beetles within the sugar cane industry.

The northern quoll is at risk from the cane toad in Australia

Since then, they have been on a slow march west across the country's northern landscape, according to the Australian government, travelling between 40 km and 60 km a year.

There are no predators or diseases that control the toads, which defend themselves using poison.

Ben Phillips, a professor at Curtin University, said the toads would reach Pilbara within two decades without action.

He said: "The toad containment zone is a once in a lifetime opportunity to stop one of the world's worst invasive species in its tracks and protect the region's unique wildlife."

#57. UK was ‘vehement’ slavery reparations should not be on Commonwealth summit agenda

British prime minister has now agreed to structured dialogue on reparatory justice but issue remains off formal Chogm agenda

British officials reportedly insisted that discussions of reparations for slavery should not be on the agenda of the Commonwealth leaders’ summit in Antigua and Barbuda – and even warned that Andy Burnham would not attend the meeting if the subject was formally included, according to a leading Caribbean political figure.

Ralph Gonsalves, a former prime minister of St Vincent and the Grenadines and a senior figure in the reparatory justice movement Repair Campaign, welcomed Britain’s decision to enter structured dialogue on reparations, but said the UK had come “kicking and screaming” to the conversation.

Currently, slavery reparations is not on the official agenda of the Commonwealth Heads of Government (Chogm) summit, which begins on Sunday.

Trade, climate resilience and finance, sustainable debt, democracy and investment in opportunities for young people are among the stated priorities for the 56-nation bloc spanning Africa, Asia, the Caribbean and the Americas, the Pacific and Europe.

“My understanding from informed circles is that Britain was vehement not to have the matter on the agenda. The British officials – and in that regard they were joined by the Canadian officials – were even raising the prospect that if it is on the agenda the prime minister of Britain wouldn’t go,” Gonsalves told the Guardian.

“They also started to circulate this untruth that India didn’t want it on the agenda, but that was not so at all. India was very supportive at the UN general assembly resolution on March 25th, 2026. So they were spreading this false word about India.”

Gonsalves said that the relentless campaign by Caribbean nations, amplified by a growing regional and global reparations movement, has propelled reparatory justice to the heart of the political conversation at the meeting.

“Because of what happened at the UN, because of what happened in Africa, what happened in the Caribbean, and all the work which is taking place in Britain itself too, inside of the British parliament, inside of the British Labour party, both in the Commons and the House of Lords, it is an issue which you can’t duck any more,” he said.

He added: “The danger of not putting it formally on the agenda is what we see happening now, that everybody who could have foreseen, whether or not it’s formally on the agenda, even the nature of how the discussions take place at the Commonwealth, and certainly in the retreats, it would have been raised.”

Gonsalves said while the actual structured dialoguewas unlikely to happen at Chogm, it was expected that when the leaders met in their private retreat, the format and details of the dialogue would be discussed.

He said: “The British government, they’re coming, kicking and screaming to the table of structured dialogue. But at least they’re coming.”

An FCDO spokesperson said: “We categorically reject this completely untrue accusation. The UK has approached this conversation in good faith, and we have at no point told any other Chogm member what should not be on an agenda.

“Our position on reparations is clear but this government respects the right of others to raise matters as they wish.”

Prof Sir Hilary Beckles, chair of the Caribbean Community (Caricom) Reparations Commission, had described the meeting as “the Chogm of reparations”, adding that reparatory justice was “the number one item” for the Caribbean as Commonwealth leaders gather in the region.

On Friday there was a historic shift in Britain’s position when the British prime minister went further than any of his predecessors by agreeing to structured dialogue and to consider an apology.

Though Caribbean governments and campaigners welcomed Burnham’s announcement, made in an op-ed for the Guardian, it was clear that agreeing on forms of reparations was still a priority for the region.

Guyana’s president, Irfaan Ali, welcomed Burnham’s statement and called for a Commonwealth subcommittee to work out the details of the dialogue over the next six to 12 months.

“I’m happy that finally the discourse has moved to a position where it should have been, where the British government is involved, because that’s the state we are dealing with,” he said.

Ali said the talks should move beyond attempting to put a price on the indignity of slavery and towards addressing its developmental consequences.

“How do we identify a system of support where the UK government, with its financial institutions, can sit with countries … and work on a development pathway, identify development gaps, whether in health, education, social services, infrastructure?”

Barbados’s prime minister, Mia Mottley, thanked Burnham for “breaking new ground”, while making clear that the two sides remained divided. “On reparations in particular, our views differ, and we will say so plainly and respectfully,” she said.

Mottley identified an apology, investment in health and education, tackling systemic poverty and inequality, and fairer debt terms among areas where agreement could be reached.

The Bahamas prime minister, Philip Davis, said the talks were not simply about “historical remembrance” but “the enduring consequences of slavery and the importance of justice, acknowledgment and meaningful redress”.

As the meeting kicks off on Sunday, the 45th anniversary of Antigua and Barbuda’s independence from British colonial rule, King Charles is indicating he will not follow Burnham and offer a full apology, there is still a sense that the reparations fight is far from over.

King Charles and Burnham will be greeted with protests as they travel through Antigua’s capital, St John’s, to attend Chogm events.

On billboards, placards and T-shirts, in letters and at a candlelight vigil for the thousands of Africans trafficked to Antigua and Barbuda during the transatlantic slave trade, the message from the Caribbean will be clear: there will be calls for an apology and reparations.

On Sunday Kemi Badenoch told broadcasters that an apology for Britain’s role in the transatlantic slave trade would be a “navel-gazing distraction”, describing slavery as an evil “in which people from every continent and region participated”.

“I would really like to sit down with my dear sister Kemi and explain to her so that she could get an understanding of what we are talking about,” Gonsalves said, adding that he would like to discuss how reparatory justice could help heal some of the fractures arising from Britain’s multicultural and ethnic tensions.

#54. US urges Venezuela to release remaining political prisoners

Venezuelan Foreign Minister Oliver Blanco and US Secretary of State Marco Rubio held talks on Monday

The US has urged Venezuela to release the remaining political prisoners being held in the South American country.

More than 1,000 political prisoners have been freed since US forces seized Venezuelan leader NicolÃ¡s Maduro in a military raid on Caracas on 3 January and took him to New York, where he faces trial on drug-trafficking and torture charges.

But Venezuelan prisoners' rights group Foro Penal says 322 people remain in detention.

US Secretary of State Marco Rubio met his Venezuelan counterpart, Oliver Blanco, on Monday where he called for their release and discussed reforms aimed at laying the groundwork for free, fair, and credible elections, the US state department said.

The release of political prisoners has long been a demand made by Venezuelan opposition leader MarÃ­a Corina Machado.

Machado, who was awarded the Nobel Peace Prize in 2025, returned to Venezuela on 25 October after living in exile for almost a year. One of her first meetings was with relatives of political detainees.

Watch: Crowds welcome Venezuela's MarÃ­a Corina Machado after months in exile

Many of those in detention were arrested in the aftermath of the 2024 presidential election.

The National Electoral Council (CNE) - which is dominated by government loyalists - declared the incumbent, NicolÃ¡s Maduro, the winner of the election even though copies of the voting tallies collected by the opposition and verified by independent media suggested that the opposition candidate had won.

The Carter Center, which had sent independent observers to Venezuela, also said that the election "could not be considered democratic".

A UN fact-finding mission found, external that in the months that followed, the Venezuelan government orchestrated "a harsh repression against persons perceived as political opponents or simply because they express dissent or critical views against the authorities".

After US forces seized Maduro, his loyal vice-president, Delcy RodrÃ­guez, was sworn in as interim president.

While RodrÃ­guez has the backing of the Trump administration, opposition parties within Venezuela have been expressing impatience with the slow pace of reforms.

In Monday's statement, the US state department stressed that it remained committed to US President Donald Trump's three-phase plan, consisting of "stabilisation, economic recovery and political reconciliation, and democratic transition".

A week ago, Rubio had told journalists that Venezuela's "election infrastructure" had to be replaced in order for fresh elections to be valid.

"Not in 10 years, not in five years, not in two years; it needs to happen quickly under the timelines that we've established," he insisted.

Days later, RodrÃ­guez told Venezuelans in a televised address that "there will be elections" under a "renewed" electoral authority.

However, she did not give a timeline for when the election could be held.

Opposition leader Machado, on the other hand, has been pushing for elections to be held in 2027.

At one point she suggested 24 July 2027 - the anniversary of the birth of independence hero SimÃ³n Bolivar - but she has since returned to calling for a vote at some point in the next year.

#52. Russian authorities detain suspect over St. Petersburg cafe blast

Our live coverage for the day has ended. Follow the latest Ukraine news here or read through the updates below.

The preventive detention hearing for Daria Trepova, who was detained in connection with the explosion at a cafe in St. Petersburg, Russia, will take place on Tuesday at Basmanny District Court in Moscow, according to Russian state news agency Vesti.

Russian authorities detained Trepova, 26, claiming she was involved in the blast that killed well-known military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky at a café in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

The US Ambassador to the United Nations Linda Thomas-Greenfield on Monday described Russia assuming the United Nations Security Council (UNSC) presidency this month as “an April Fool’s joke.”

“But the truth of the matter is, it’s a rotating seat. We expect that they will behave professionally. But we also expect that they will use their seat to spread disinformation and to promote their own agenda as it relates to Ukraine, and we will stand ready to call them out at every single moment that they attempt to do that,” Greenfield added.

Greenfield said she is not surprised if the Russians asked the foreign minister to come to UNSC headquarters in New York but also said council does work beyond Ukraine.

“We haven’t decided yet on what our attendance levels will be, but we intend to carry out the business of the Security Council during this month. The Security Council does more than Ukraine. We work on many issues, and we again expect that Russia will carry their presidency in a professional way, but when they don’t, we will stand ready to call them out,” Greenfield said.

Vasily Nebenzya, Russian ambassador to the United Nations, dismissed concerns that Russia could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the ongoing war in Ukraine.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded and pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Newly published video shows the moments running up to an explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladen Tartovsky in a St Petersburg café on Sunday.

Tatarsky died when a blast tore through the cafe where he was appearing as a guest of a pro-war group called Cyber Front Z. Prior Russian media reports suggested that Tatarsky may have been killed by a device hidden in a figurine presented to him by a woman before the blast.

The 25-second video shows Tartovsky putting a statuette given to him at the event into a box after passing the microphone to another man.

He places the statuette back into the box and then covers it with what appears to be paper wrapping. As he presses down the paper, there is an explosion.

In the last two seconds of the video, Tartovsky is pixillated, but the rest of the scene is not.

More to know: The well-known Russian blogger was killed in what appeared to be an audacious attack on a high-profile pro-Kremlin figure, officials said.

Authorities said they were treating the case as suspected murder.

At least 32 people were injured in the blast, with 10 people in serious condition, state media Ria Novosti reported, citing the Russian Ministry of Health.

Russian ambassador to the United Nations Vasily Nebenzya on Monday scoffed at concerns that his country could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the war in Ukraine.

Nebenzya pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Ukraine has received the first tranche of $2.7 billion from a new International Monetary Fund (IMF) program, Ukrainian Finance Minister Sergii Marchenko said in a tweet Monday.

The IMF, which regularly makes emergency loans to countries in crisis, on Friday approved a new four-year extended arrangement under the Extended Fund Facility (EFF) of around $15.6 billion as part of a $115 billion total support package for Ukraine.

The program aims to “anchor policies that sustain fiscal, external, price and financial stability and support economic recovery, while enhancing governance and strengthening institutions to promote long-term growth in the context of post-war reconstruction and Ukraine’s path to EU accession,” the IMF said in a statement.

The program also helps Ukraine to carry out “more ambitious structural reforms,” it said.

The Extended Fund Facility (EFF) loan is the first major conventional financing program approved by the IMF for a country involved in a large-scale war, Reuters reported.

The risks to the arrangement are “exceptionally high,” said Gita Gopinath, first deputy managing director of the global lender.

“The success of the program depends on the size, composition, and timing of external financing on concessional terms to help close fiscal and external financing gaps and restore debt sustainability on a forward-looking basis under the baseline and downside scenarios,” she added.

German Chancellor Olaf Scholz on Monday assured Moldova of Berlin’s support on its path to accession to the European Union as Moldovan and American officials allege Russia of trying to weaken the government in Chisinau.

“Moldova is part of our European family. In the summer, we granted it candidate status. And I very much welcome how resolutely Moldova has tackled the necessary reforms that are indispensable for EU accession,” Scholz told a news conference in Bucharest, Romania alongside Romanian President Klaus Iohannis and Moldovan President Maia Sandu.

“Moldova can be sure of our support on this path. I assured the President [Sandu] of this once again today. Moldova does not stand alone, but receives massive international support,” the German chancellor continued.

Scholz expressed “great concern” about reports of alleged Russian attempts to destabilize Moldova and said Germany would do its “utmost” to support Moldova in arming itself against “attempts of destabilization by Russia.”

In February, Moldova’s President Sandu accused Russia of planning to use “saboteurs who have undergone military training and are disguised as civilians” to destabilize the country — claims which were rejected by Russia’s foreign ministry as “unfounded.”

According to White House officials, the US believes that Russia is working to weaken the Moldovan government, as it continues to seek closer ties with the European Union.

“The sovereignty and territorial integrity of any state is inviolable. This requirement of the Helsinki Final Act and other agreements under international law was also signed by Russia. And it is still valid. Therefore, we do our utmost to support Moldova in arming itself against attempts of destabilization by Russia,” Scholz said.

Speaking at the same event, Sandu said that “it is very important, and I am happy that Moldova is a dialogue partner with Romania and Germany. The projects we are involved in together are very useful for our people and will strongly lead us to the accession (to the European Union).”

CNN’s Radina Gigova, Anna Chernova and Natasha Bertrand contributed reporting to this post.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said Monday that the alliance has not seen any changes in Russia’s nuclear posture since Russian President Vladimir Putin announced late last month Moscow’s plans to station tactical nuclear weapons in neighboring Belarus.

“We monitor very closely what Russia does. But so far, we haven’t seen any changes in their nuclear posture that requires any change in our nuclear posture,” the NATO chief said, while answering questions from reporters on the topic at a news conference in Brussels.

Stoltenberg described Putin’s announcement as a “pattern of dangerous, reckless nuclear rhetoric.”

“President Putin tries to use nuclear weapons as a way to prevent us from supporting Ukraine… intimidation, coercion to stop NATO Allies and partners for supporting Ukraine in their right to defend their own country,” he said.

“We will not be intimidated. We will continue to support Ukraine,” Stoltenberg continued.

NATO’s chief Jens Stoltenberg said on Monday that Western allies have delivered more than $70 billion in military aid to Ukraine over the last year since the Russian invasion started.

“There are no signs that President Putin is preparing for peace. He is preparing for more war,” Stoltenberg warned, speaking at a news conference in Brussels.

“We do not know when this war will end. But when it does, we will need to put in place arrangements so that Ukraine can deter future aggression. And history does not repeat itself. We cannot allow Russia to continue to chip away at European security,” he said.

Stoltenberg added that allies have delivered 65 billion euros (around $70.7 billion) in military aid, and said he welcomed the recent arrival of modern battle tanks and other armored vehicles in Ukraine.

“This can make a real difference on the front lines and allow the Ukrainian forces to liberate more territory,” he said.

The NATO head affirmed that the alliance’s support is for the “long haul” and said, “we will discuss how we can step up our support, including by continuing to strengthen Ukraine’s armed forces and supporting the transition from Soviet-era to NATO equipment and doctrine.”

Stoltenberg also said allies must address other threats and challenges, citing instability, terrorism, and the growing influence of Iran, Russia, and China. He urged allies to invest more in defense to tackle all these wide range of issues.

Norway and Denmark will cooperate to donate 8,000 artillery rounds to Ukraine, the Norwegian government announced in a statement on Monday.

“Norway donates the shells, while Denmark donates the associated fuzes, propellant bags and primer cartridges,” the statement said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky reacted to the announcement, saying in a tweet that he was grateful for the donation.

“Grateful to the Danish and Norwegian governments led by @Statsmin and @jonasgahrstore for the joint initiative to transfer an additional batch of artillery shells to Ukraine. Timely military assistance from partners is the key to bringing our joint victory closer!” Zelensky said.

The rounds will be “standard NATO 155 mm artillery ammunition and will supplement the Danish donation of 19 Caesar self-propelled howitzers,” Norway’s statement added.

Norway’s artillery shells will be delivered from the country’s armed forces’ own stock, according to the statement.

Norwegian defense minister Bjørn Arild Gram said “Ukraine has a significant need for artillery ammunition. Norway will contribute where we can. It is important for both Europe’s and Norway’s security that Ukraine succeeds in standing up to Russia’s attack.”

“The Ukrainians still have a great and urgent need for ammunition for their fight for freedom against Russia. Denmark, together with other EU and NATO countries, is in the process of accommodating with a number of different military contributions. Our cooperation with Norway is an important example of this,” the Danish acting defense minister, Troels Lund Poulsen, said in the statement.

The Ukrainian military has reported little change to the front lines but heavy fire from Russian forces at various parts of the front line in the Donetsk region.

The General Staff said that more than 45 enemy attacks had been repelled over the past day, with the focus on Lyman, Bakhmut, Avdiivka and Mariinka.

It said there were 17 air strikes and more than 20 strikes by multiple launch rocket systems.

“In the Bakhmut direction, the enemy is trying to take full control over the city of Bakhmut and continues to storm it,” the General Staff said.

West of the city, Ukrainian defense forces repelled about 20 enemy attacks, according to the General Staff.

Oleksandr Syrskyi, commander of Ukrainian Land Forces, posted on Telegram that in Bakhmut he had met with “soldiers and commanders who destroyed the myth of invincibility of the Wagner fighters and Russian paratroopers.”

“The enemy is weakening and trying to cover up its failures with new fakes about the capture of Bakhmut,” Syrskyi said.

Syrskyi referenced the possibility of a future counter-offensive, saying that “the forward movement of our entire army depends on what every soldier is doing right now on their section of the front.”

Some of the most intense fighting is to the northwest and west of the city of Donetsk – as the Russians continue months-long efforts to dislodge Ukrainian defenses in Avdiivka and Mariinka. The General Staff said that some 20 assaults had been repelled.

“There are also some operational successes of our military in the Donetsk area. Our military have taken some positions. They have taken a more strategic and favourable position,” said Oleksii Dmytrashkivskyi, spokesperson for the military in that zone.

He also noted that Russian special forces had been introduced in the area.

“The enemy is trying to attack our positions between Avdiivka and Marinka. These attempts do not stop. It should be noted that the enemy has suffered quite significant losses,” Dmytrashkivskyi said.

He also claimed that the Russians “don’t have the same amount of equipment and, as they themselves say, they are on a ‘diet’ of ammunition for assaults.”

Social media video showed both sides engaged in heavy fire in the Avdiivka area, which the Russians have surrounded on three sides.

Denis Pushilin, the leader of the self-declared Donetsk People’s Republic, claimed that Russian forces were still advancing in the Avdiivka direction.

“It is still premature to speak of encirclement, but the situation is being created, if not critical, then extremely difficult for the enemy… After clearing Kamianka, our units are moving further. There are difficulties both with the terrain, which must also be overcome, and there are also similarities with weather conditions. But these difficulties are mutual — both for the enemy and for us,” Pushilin said.

The war in Ukraine has cost an estimated $2.6 billion of damage to heritage and cultural sites in the country, the UN cultural body UNESCO said in a recently published report.

The report, which covers one year of war in Ukraine between February 24, 2022, and February 24, 2023, was a joint assessment conducted by the Ukrainian government, the World Bank, the European Commission, and the United Nations.

UNESCO was in charge of the part of the report dedicated to culture and tourism.

“As of February 24, 2023, the total damage cost from identified assets is estimated at $2.6 billion,” the report said in its assessment of damage related to culture and tourism.

Some $1.7 billion of that cost was distributed to “historic cities, buildings, and sites imbued with recognized cultural/social values,” $650 million to tourism facilities, $143 million to “movable cultural properties and collections, repositories of culture,” and $150 million to “buildings/workshops/ateliers dedicated to cultural and creative industries,” according to UNESCO.

The most impacted region in Ukraine was Kharkivska, which has suffered 30% of the total damage to its cultural assets, the report added.

UNESCO said that losses are estimated at around $15.2 billion, which includes revenue losses from tourism, art, sports, entertainment, recreation, cultural education, and creative and cultural industries.

“The war has significantly impacted the diversity and richness of culture and cultural heritage in Ukraine, causing damage to cultural infrastructure and assets, reducing livelihoods for cultural creators, bearers and practitioners, limiting access to culture, and impeding the exercise of cultural rights,” the report said.

UNESCO’s head Audrey Azoulay is currently in Ukraine, the organization told CNN on Monday.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said it will be historic when the military alliance raises the Finnish flag for the first time at its headquarters on Tuesday.

“This is a historic week. Tomorrow we will welcome Finland as the 31st member of NATO, making Finland safer and our alliance stronger. We will raise the Finnish flag for the first time here at NATO Headquarters,” Stoltenberg said, speaking in Brussels Monday.

“It will be a good day for Finland’s security, for Nordic security, and for NATO as a whole,” Stoltenberg added.

Finland is set to become a member of the alliance on Tuesday, with Finnish President Sauli Niinistö traveling to Brussels to attend an accession ceremony at NATO headquarters.

Stoltenberg also spoke about Sweden’s bid to join NATO, saying Finland’s accession is “in itself something we should celebrate” but is also good for Sweden.

“It makes Sweden more integrated into NATO and makes Sweden even more safer,” Stoltenberg said, adding “at the same time we celebrate and enjoy that Finland is now full-fledged member, we should continue work to finalize the Swedish accession process.”

CNN’s Laurent Kent contributed to this report.

#49. Fossilised red blood cells found in 115m-year-old dinosaur’s bone, say scientists

Chemical tests on Aratasaurus bone identify presence of heme, the organic compound that allows haemoglobin in blood to carry oxygen around body

It could be the opening of a Jurassic Park rip-off: scientists examining ancient dinosaur bones find what appear to be fossilised red blood cells, and traces of the molecule that gives the beast’s blood its distinctive crimson hue.

But that is what researchers are claiming to have discovered after analysing the hind leg bones of a small, meat-eating dinosaur called an Aratasaurus that lived and died in what is now north-eastern Brazil 115m years ago.

Electron microscope images of the dinosaur bones revealed small, round structures that resemble red blood cells in fine channels where blood vessels once ran, while chemical tests identified the presence of heme, the organic compound that allows haemoglobin in blood to carry oxygen around the body.

Such claims are controversial because biological tissues and molecules can break down fast, leaving little or no trace in ancient fossils. But if confirmed, it would open up entirely new avenues for scientists, allowing them to investigate dinosaur blood, metabolism and even their activity levels.

“We are seeing a mineral and molecular echo of the dinosaur’s original biology,” said Prof Kliti Grice at Curtin University in Western Australia who led the research team. “Fossilised red blood cells with distinct chemistry related to heme have never been found in situ in any fossil, let alone a dinosaur. This is a world first.”

The fossilised remnants of the Aratasaurus were discovered in a mine in 2008 and have since been held in the National Museum of Brazil, where researchers continue to study them. While analysing the dinosaur’s hind leg bones, Grice spotted the structures that resembled red blood cells.

The scientists’ suspicions that they were looking at red blood cells were bolstered when chemical tests came back positive for heme and its breakdown products. Tests also found traces of the amino acid building blocks for collagen, a key protein in connective and other tissues.

If the structures really are fossilised red blood cells, as the researchers contend, it raises the question of how they were preserved. According to Grice, a manganese compound called hausmannite, which formed in and around the blood cells, may have lent a helping hand by stabilising them prior to fossilisation. The same process might have helped preserve the amino acids, she added. The study appears in Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences.

The work is already stoking debate. Prof Mary Schweitzer at North Carolina State University in the US, who has discovered remnants of blood vessels and soft tissues in dinosaur fossils, is not convinced Grice and her colleagues have found red blood cells. One reason for her doubt is that the dinosaur’s red blood cells should have a “nuclear bulge” making them appear dome-like on both sides. The structures Grice found are concave, suggesting they might be something else.

Schweitzer was more persuaded by the discovery of heme and collagen amino acids in the bone and said there was so much more to learn from exceptionally well-preserved fossils. Schweitzer added: “The conventional wisdom in palaeontology that all endogenous organics would disappear in a million years or less is being routinely challenged, and will continue to be as technological approaches to palaeontological specimens are more routinely applied.”

Another question the research raises is what other biological material might lurk in fossils collections around the world. On this, Grice urges extreme caution, adding that her work does not imply that large biological molecules such as proteins or DNA live on in ancient fossils. “Any extraordinary claim needs extraordinary analytical evidence,” she said. “The aim isn’t to find Jurassic Park DNA, it’s to determine how much genuine molecular information deep time can preserve.”

“The really exciting implication is that extraordinary molecular information may already be sitting in museum collections around the world, waiting for us to ask new questions of old fossils,” she added.

#48. Tenerife striker De Miguel dies after on-field collision

De Miguel died in hospital following serious injuries suffered in on-pitch collision

Tenerife striker Jesus de Miguel has died after the 30-year-old suffered a serious injury during a Spanish second-division match.

The forward suffered head and chest injuries during a match against FC Andorra on Monday in a heavy collision with goalkeeper Jesus Owono, who was sent off for his challenge.

De Miguel's condition was described as 'stable but serious' on Monday but his health deteriorated on Tuesday morning, with the Spaniard dying at 07:00 GMT.

Reuters reported on Tuesday that police in Spain have opened an investigation for possible involuntary manslaughter following De Miguel's death.

Under Spanish law, any death that is deemed non-natural automatically triggers a judicial inquiry.

"Heartbroken, with a lump in our throats and unable to believe this is true" Tenerife said.

"There are no words of comfort; there is absolutely nothing that can lessen this overwhelming sadness. We stand with his grieving family, with his loved ones, with all of Tenerife who mourn this cruel loss today."

Jesus de Miguel joined Tenerife in 2025 on a three-year deal

Tenerife and Andorra were drawing 2-2 seven minutes into stoppage time when the incident occurred.

Owono ran towards the centre circle to clear a long ball when his knee appeared to collide with De Miguel.

The striker lay motionless on the pitch as medical staff treated him before he was taken by ambulance from Tenerife's Estadio Heliodoro Rodriguez Lopez to hospital.

Players from both teams were visibly distressed by the incident, with a number of De Miguel's team-mates in tears.

The referee showed Owono a straight red card before abandoning the match.

The goalkeeper, who had over 30,000 followers on Instagram on Tuesday morning, has since deactivated his account.

Spanish media, including Marca, reported De Miguel suffered a cardiac arrest in the ambulance and was resuscitated by paramedics on the way to hospital.

Tenerife's president, Felipe Minambres, says the club's players will need "professional assistance" to help deal with the ordeal of one of their team-mates dying.

"We are all feeling vulnerable and deeply saddened right now. It's hard to even believe what has happened," said Minambres.

"We know, especially the players and the players' partners, that we will need support. We've already begun receiving offers of help today, and that support will continue.

"We will need professional assistance, but we also need the support of all our fans, because they are likely feeling the same way we are today."

Minambres added it had been a "truly unbelievable situation" and "an enormous tragedy that we have had to endure".

Andorra, Tenerife's opponents on Monday, issued a statement later on Tuesday which spoke of the club's shock and expressed condolences to De Miguel's family, club "and all those who were close to him".

"We are deeply affected by what has happened and we share the pain that the club is experiencing," it continued.

"From the very beginning we have maintained contact with them and we are at their disposal for anything they may need.

"What has happened has had a huge emotional impact on our players, the coaching staff and everyone at FC Andorra. These are particularly difficult times for everyone and we want to make sure that no one has to face them alone. That is why the club is providing psychological support to anyone who needs it."

Former Barcelona defender Gerard Pique, who co-owns Andorra, said: "I am sorry with all my soul. Rest in peace, Jesus."

De Miguel's former club, Castellon, said: "It's hard to find words when reality strikes in such an unfair way.

"We join with all our affection in the grief of his family and loved ones, whom we embrace in these terribly difficult moments and to whom we extend our most heartfelt condolences."

Javier Tebas, president of La Liga, said: "Today, we all share the terrible pain of an irreparable loss.

"My deepest condolences to his family, loved ones, team-mates and the whole club."

Real Madrid and Arsenal honour De Miguel with minute's silence

Real Madrid and France striker Kylian Mbappe wrote on his social media: "Much sadness, there are no words."

Inter Miami and former Barcelona forward Lionel Messi said: "There are no words. My condolences and lots of strength to all his family and friends."

The Spanish football federation announced following De Miguel's death that Tuesday's Spanish Cup fixtures had been postponed.

Two fixtures in the first round of the men's Copa del Rey were due to be played, while Espanyol were set to take on AS Celtas in the women's Copa del Reina competition.

De Miguel scored 11 goals in 29 matches for Tenerife

De Miguel, who was born in Madrid, started his career at Leganes in 2015 and went on to play for eight different clubs in Spain.

After spells with Lugo Fuenlabrada and Internacional Madrid, he returned to Fuenlabrada before stints at Union Adarve, Navalcarnero and Unionistas.

He joined Castellon in 2023 and scored 16 goals as the club achieved promotion to Spain's second division a year later.

The Spaniard joined Tenerife on a three-year deal in 2025 and scored 11 goals in 29 appearances for the side.

De Miguel is survived by his daughter, Carla de Miguel Fernandez, who was born in 2025.

Arsenal's squad observed a minute's silence for De Miguel before training on Tuesday

#42. Irish Olympian Ciara Mageean was a 'talented and inspirational human being'

Ciara Mageean's funeral took place in Portaferry, County Down

Olympian Ciara Mageean has been described as a "truly remarkable, immensely talented and inspirational human being" in a tribute read out at her funeral.

Hundreds of people have paid their respects to the runner at her funeral on Tuesday.

The former European champion and two-time Olympic runner died on Sunday after she was diagnosed with stage four bowel cancer.

Among those in attendance at the funeral in Portaferry, County Down, were Northern Ireland First Minister Michelle O'Neill, as well as fellow Olympian Rhys McClenaghan and 1972 gold medallist Lady Mary Peters.

Mageean's funeral cortege was given a guard of honour by Portaferry GAC and her former school, Assumption Grammar in Ballynahinch.

Her death comes less than three weeks after her marriage to her long-term partner Thomas Moran.

Mourners began arriving at around midday for the 2:00 GMT requiem Mass at St Patrick's Church.

Family, friends and those wishing to pay their respects followed the funeral cortege the short distance along the Ballyphilip Road from Mageean's family home to the church.

Others in attendance included representatives for Irish President Catherine Connolly and the Irish Government, Social Democratic and Labour Party leader Claire Hanna, GAA President Jarlath Burns and former boxer Paddy Barnes.

European champion Ciara Mageean died surrounded by her loved ones at the weekend

The Mass booklet included an image of Mageean's greatest achievement on the track, the moment she crossed the line at the 2024 European Championships.

In his homily, Father Martin Kelly, who knew Mageean from her childhood, spoke of her accomplishments, her love for her husband and the joy and pride she had brought to her hometown.

Kelly recalled visiting her in hospice shortly before her death, when she suggested they go for a walk.

Despite her illness, Mageean noticed he was struggling and offered to link arms with him.

"On the way down, she knew I was struggling and she said, 'Let me link you'," he told mourners.

"One of my happiest moments and memories is that of Ciara Mageean linking my arm as we walked back to her room."

The athlete represented Ireland at the 2016 and 2020 Olympics and won gold in the 1500m at the European Athletics Championships in Rome in 2024.

She also represented Northern Ireland at the Commonwealth Games, winning silver in Birmingham in 2022.

Noticing blood in your poo - which may appear black or dark red in the toilet bowl - can be a sign of bowel cancer.

If there is no other obvious cause for the bleeding, such as piles, the NHS recommends that you ask for an appointment with your GP, external.

The NHS also advises a check-up if you notice changes in your regular toilet habits, such as having to poo more often, or a pattern of diarrhoea or constipation.

Abdominal pain or lumps, stomach bloating and unexplained weight loss are also among symptoms which should lead to a bowel cancer test.

Bowel cancer can also cause anaemia, the features of which include extreme tiredness and shortness of breath.

The earlier bowel cancer is detected, the easier it is to treat.
